## (d) THIS RANGE'S OWN CRUCES, BY COLUMN

### 0298B — *nihil mundamini* (0021)
*Sive vitatis mala sive facitis bona, nihil mundamini, sed solum adventum Redemptoris
exspectetis.* Rendered **"you are cleansed in nothing"**. *mundamini* is a well-formed passive,
but "whether you shun evils or do good, you are cleansed in nothing" argues against the sentence's
own second limb. A conjecture that construes is *nihil moremini*, "delay not at all, but await
only the coming of the Redeemer" — one minim's difference. **No marker**: the printed word is
real, the sentence is obscure rather than false, and Pattern 18a keeps that class in the crux.
Logged for the plate (§e.1).

### 0298B — the Lc 12:36 lemma, `[var:]` FIRED
See §c.3. The gloss's *judici pulsanti* expounds words the printed lemma does not carry.

### 0298D — *exteriorum damnatorum similitudinem* (0021)
Rendered **"the likeness of outward condemned men"**. The simile actually introduced is the
householder and the thief, i.e. **outward losses**, which is *damnorum*; *damnatorum* is a real
word and is rendered as one. No marker (§e.2).

### 0299C — the anacoluthon at VERS. 43 (0021)
*Iste autem qui repertus fuerit fideliter annonam verbi ministrat, et in curam gregis vigilans,
aeterna beatitudine glorificabitur.* The relative clause takes an indicative *ministrat* where the
construction wants a participle, so the sentence has two finite verbs and no clean subject. The
English carries the join as printed ("he who shall have been found faithfully ministers … and,
watching …, shall be glorified"). Nothing is emended (§e.3).

### 0300A, 0300B — *usque ad* used four ways in two columns
Two are the recension's splice formula inside patristic extracts (0299C AMBR., 0308D AUG.); two
are ordinary prepositional uses in the gloss's own prose (*usque ad finem saeculi*, *usque ad
perfectionem*). **All four render "as far as"** per §1.6, which reads naturally in each. Recorded
because a later sweep looking for the formula will find four hits in this range and only two are
the formula.

### 0301B — *ne contemptus te accuset* (0021), a mechanism-5 site
*contemptus* is nominative masculine and agrees with **sermo Dei**, not with *te*. The English
reads **"lest, being despised, it accuse thee"** — the word of God accusing, not the reader being
accused for contempt. The easier reading here is the wrong one, which is the class the brief names
as dominant.

### 0301D — *petierunt* for *perierunt* (0022)
*nuntiatur ei de Galilaeis, quod quasi juste ideo petierunt, quod non bene se praeparaverunt.*
Rendered **"who as it were justly sought this for the cause that…"**. The same sentence continues
*et illos vere fuisse peccatores et **juste periisse***, and the next clause *alios peccatores
**perituros***, so *perierunt* is what the argument wants — one letter away. **No marker, no
`[cj:]`**: the printed word is real, and internal corroboration proves an error exists, never
whose it is (§e.4).

### 0302A — *ex iota et ita* (0022)
The Greek for eighteen is ιη´, iota + **eta**. Migne prints *ita*, which is also the ordinary
medieval Latin NAME of the letter η (Byzantine *ita*), so this is very possibly correct as
printed. Rendered as printed, **"by iota and ita"**, and flagged only so a plate reader can
confirm the sort (§e.5).

### 0302B — *Vinea Domini sabaoh* (0022)
A **non-word**: *sabaoh* for *sabaoth*. It sits inside an italic lemma, where a second italic
cannot nest, and it is carried as printed. It could be Migne's dropped final sort or a Corpus
Corporum loss; **I do not know which and assert neither.** No `[sic:]` (§e.6). The Vulgate's own
*exercituum* at Is 5:7 is discussed at §c, span 46, and took no `[var:]`.

### 0302B — the Is 9:2 proof-text, `[var:]` FIRED
See §c, span 45. Three words replaced in a clause carrying its own address.

### 0302C — *in Synagoga crebris docet* (0022)
*crebris* is an ablative plural adjective with no noun; the sense wants *crebris [sermonibus]* or
an adverb. Rendered **"teaches often in the Synagogue"**, which is the least that the printed
words allow without supplying a noun. Logged (§e.7).

### 0303C — *…curvata fuit, quae magis terrena quam coelestia noverat, operatur.* (0022)
Two finite verbs, the second stranded at the end of the sentence with no subject of its own.
Rendered as printed, with *operatur* left where Migne sets it: **"…was bent away from the
uprightness of her mind, she who had known earthly things rather than heavenly, works."** The
conjecture that construes is *noverat **operari*** ("knew how to work earthly things rather than
heavenly"). **No marker** (§e.8).

### 0304A — a paragraph with no terminal stop (0022)
VERS. 16 ends *…quod est filiam Abrahae a vinculo noxiae inclinationis erigi* with **no full
stop**. Pattern 8: none supplied. The English paragraph ends the same way, mid-air.

### 0304B — *Deum de sua erectione glorificantem* (0022), mechanism-5 checked
The participle is accusative feminine and agrees with *filiam Abrahae, id est Ecclesiam* — so it
is **the Church** that glorifies God for her raising up, not the rejoicing bystanders, who are the
easier antecedent. Rendered accordingly.

### 0306C — `Ὕδωρ`, the only Greek in the work (0024)
*Ὕδωρ , aqua, inde hydropis, morbus aquosus subcutaneus.* The Greek is reproduced **verbatim, in
Greek**, with the space Migne sets before the comma. *hydropis* is set as a nominative where it is
the genitive of *hydrops*; the English renders it **"the dropsy"**, the one English word that
keeps the ὕδωρ derivation visible (dropsy < hydropsy < ὕδρωψ), so that the etymology the sentence
exists to make still works in the English. The choice is recorded rather than silent (§e.10).

### 0307A — the Rom 3:23 proof-text, `[var:]` FIRED; and `(Rom. XXXV)`
*gratia* for *gloria* — see §c, span 96. The note's address names a chapter Romans does not have;
reproduced verbatim, unmarked (§e.15).

### 0307A/B — the Ps 87:16 proof-text, `[var:]` FIRED
*confusus* for *conturbatus* — see §c, span 100.

### 0307B — *Honoratiori post invitato locum dat, qui … praeitur* (0024), mechanism-5 site
*qui* is the man who **gives** place — the over-confident earlier guest — and *praeitur* is
passive, so he **is outstripped** by the nimbleness of those who followed Christ. The easier
reading binds *qui* to the more honourable newcomer and makes the verb active; that reading is
wrong on both the case and the voice. Rendered per the morphology.

### 0309B — *domum intellectus accipiunt* (0024)
*Quidam autem vocantur et contemnunt, qui domum intellectus accipiunt, sed opere non implent.*
Rendered **"who receive the house of understanding"**. The parallel three lines later —
*qui acceptam **intelligentiam** opere adimplent* — wants ***donum*** *intellectus*, "the gift of
understanding," and *domum*/*donum* differ by one sort. Real word, so no `[sic:]`; not a negation
or correlative, so no `[cj:]`. Logged (§e.11).

### 0309C — *cognitione conjunctos* (0024)
*ut et cognitione conjunctos, et divino praecepto diligamus.* Rendered **"those joined to us by
acquaintance"**. The passage is about hating father, mother, wife and children while loving them,
so the expected word is ***cognatione***, "by kinship". One letter. No marker (§e.12).

### 0310B — *piorum affectum* (0024)
*legationem lacrymarum, bonorum operum, piorum affectum* — three genitives, and the third is an
accusative singular where the series requires *affectuum*. English has no exponent for the case,
so the rendering **"of holy affection"** is unavoidable and the defect is invisible in the English;
that is exactly why it is logged here (§e.13).

### 0310B — the 1 Cor 4:9 quotation, `[var:]` DECLINED
Order and connectives only; see §c, span 137. The address `(II Cor. IV)` is wrong for the verse.

### 0310C — *spiritualia sapientia* (0025)
*ad condienda corda auditorum spiritualia sapientia*: a neuter plural adjective against an ablative
singular noun. Rendered **"with spiritual wisdom"**; Pattern 9 governs — English carries no
exponent for the disagreement, there is nothing to emend and nothing to mark (§e.14).

### Mechanism-5 census — ALL FIVE CHUNKS, not just the first two
The brief's warning that a stint's apparatus is silent about what it never looked for is answered
here explicitly. Every site in this range where a pronoun, relative or participle could bind to
either of two nouns was checked on case and gender rather than on sense:
**0021** — *ne contemptus te accuset* (0301B, above) · *a quo liberatur* (0301B: *quo* = *sermo
Dei*, the adversary of the lemma, not the judge) · *qui cum in se nil haberet* (0300B: *qui* = the
Lord, not the devotion) · *quem judex exactori … tradet* (0301B: *quem* = the sinner).
**0022** — *quam tamen non ex toto exstirpandam* (0303B: *quam* = the Synagogue) ·
*Deum de sua erectione glorificantem* (0304B, above) · *quorum … uterque* (0304A: the two peoples)
· *quae … vincula … erupit* (0303D–0304A: the Church, not the soul).
**0023** — *quam in die resurrectionis glorificatam* (0306A: *quam* = the Church, his body) ·
*cui destructioni eorum … condoleo* (0306A: the destruction, not the prophets).
**0024** — *qui … praeitur* (0307B, above) · *cum vix illum praeparatum … discutit* (0310B: God is
the subject of *discutit*, *illum* the object; the easier reading makes the man the examiner) ·
*quos habemus ad malum suasores* (0310A).
**0025** — *ne in eis germen oriretur* (0310C: *eis* = the salted cities).
No re-binding was found that the English had taken the easy way on; the two named above (0301B,
0307B) are the sites where the easy reading was available and refused.

### What I would most want read at the plate, in order
1. **0302B *sabaoh*** — a bare non-word, and the only one in the range. cols 301/302 = archive.org
   leaf n157 (`leaf n = (column + 11) / 2`).
2. **0301D *petierunt*** — same leaf as item 1, so one read settles both.
3. **0309B *domum*** and **0309C *cognitione*** — adjacent columns, one leaf (n160), and both are
   one-sort differences from a word the context supplies.
4. **0298B *mundamini*** (leaf n155) and **0298D *damnatorum*** (same leaf).
5. **0302A *ita*** — free, it is on the leaf of items 1–2; a confirmed *ita* closes the question and
   a printed *eta* opens a different one.

⚑ **Six of these seventeen would look, from our file alone, like exactly the sites a `[sic:]` or a
`[cj:]` belongs on.** None is marked. On John that discipline is what produced every correction the
work received, and the same wager is being made here.

# STINT 0026 — chunks 0026–0030, cols 0310C–0325C

*Preserved verbatim from `cruces-0026.md`. Where it states a `[var:]` threshold of its own, §0.1 governs.*

# Cruces — 9000, *Glossa ordinaria*, **Evangelium secundum Lucam**, chunks 0026–0030

Stint 6 of eight. **Chunks 0026–0030, cols 0310C–0325C, CAPUT XV–XVIII** — Luke's parable core:
the lost sheep and the lost drachma, the prodigal son, the unjust steward, Dives and Lazarus, the
ten lepers, the coming of the Son of man, the unjust judge, the Pharisee and the publican, the
rich ruler, and blind Bartimaeus.

Sections in the order the launch brief requires: **(a)** the policy, before the findings ·
**(b)** the span count against the brief's 191 · **(c)** the lemma divergence list, all 191 spans
· **(d)** this range's cruces by column · **(e)** the declined-oddity list, gathered in one place
for the plate reader.

---

## (a) THE POLICY THIS STINT APPLIED — stated before the findings so it can be argued with

### a.1 Markers

- **`[sic: …]` fired: ZERO.** Not one, in 5,985 Latin words. This is the brief's headline
  instruction and I have obeyed it literally: **every suspect word in my range is in §(e),
  unmarked, with its column, and with no claim about whose the defect is.** Where I have a
  conjecture I give it and I say plainly that a conjecture proves an error exists and never
  proves who made it. Two of my nineteen have on-page controls that read as decisive
  (*amore* at 0315B, *praecepta* at 0316B); I have written both down **as controls that do not
  settle anything**, because that is exactly the reasoning the brief records as running right 5 /
  wrong 9 on 9004.
- **`[cj: …]` fired: ZERO.** Four sites in my range meet Pattern 18's shape — a real, well-formed
  Latin word whose faithful English misleads (*negatur* 0312A, *amore* 0315B, *irritus* 0316A,
  *caecum* 0325A). I declined all four. My reason is 18a's own test: the bar is whether the
  English **asserts something false**, and each of these produces a sentence that is *odd or
  unconstruable* rather than one that asserts a falsehood a reader would carry away. An odd
  sentence is visible to the reader; a false one is not. They are in §(e) instead.
- **`[var: …]` fired: TWO**, both citable against `sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt` by book
  chapter:verse, both a whole word of the received text replaced or removed rather than clipped:
  **0321C** *In illa die* (Lc 17:31 *In illa hora*) and **0322A** *qui habent tanquam non
  habentes* (1 Cor 7:29 *qui habent **uxores**, tamquam non habentes sint*).
- **`[d:]`, `[ed:]`, `[nt:]`, `[cn:]` fired: ZERO.** No dittography, no lost text in the
  digitization, no note whose content is prose (all sixteen `[n:]` in my range are sigla or
  citations), no foot-of-page apparatus.

### a.2 The line I drew on `[var:]`, so the twenty declines below can be argued with

`[var:]` **fires** where a whole word or clause of the received text is **replaced by different
matter, or removed from inside the quotation**, in a way that changes what the verse says.
`[var:]` is **declined**, and the divergence logged here instead, where the difference is:

  (i) a **clip** at either end of the quotation — Migne's citing practice throughout this
      recension, and the commonest single class in my range;
  (ii) an **inflection, a single letter, or a word order** — the class in which Corpus Corporum
      can manufacture a Vulgate divergence that a collation then dutifully "finds"
      (the `judicabit`→`judicavit` trap), and the class in which English frequently has **no
      exponent at all**, so the marker would be the only place the claim lived;
  (iii) an **adaptation to the citing sentence** — case, person, mood, or a finite verb made out
      of an infinitive so the verse can sit inside the gloss's own syntax.

⚑ The two markers I did fire are neither (i) nor (ii) nor (iii). *hora*→*die* is one substantive
noun exchanged for another at the verse being glossed; the loss of *uxores* is a word taken out
of the **middle** of a clause, which changes the object of *habent* from wives to possessions
generally — and it is the second sense the gloss then argues from.

### a.3 The inherited conventions (brief §1), and one exception

All six were applied from the first line, and each is audited in §(d) against the Latin rather
than by impression. **One exception was forced, and the Latin that forced it is at
0315D/0316A — see §(d), CHUNK 0027.** It is *usque ad* inside Luke 16:16 itself.

### a.4 Two places the English departs from what a reader might expect, declared up front

1. **Two non-words are carried into the English in italics, untranslated, and NOT marked** —
   *fideres* @0321A and *Testatamenti* @0324D. Pattern 7/10 says a non-word is carried in
   italics; Pattern 12 would normally wrap it in `[sic:]`; this brief forbids the marker. I have
   followed the brief. **The cost is that chunk 0028 has 47 italic spans against the Latin's 46,
   and chunk 0030 has 47 against 46.** Both deltas are these two runs and nothing else. They are
   declared here so the delta is not read downstream as a structural defect, and so the merge can
   overrule me in one edit if it prefers the marker.
2. **Two supplied English words stand in square brackets** — "he [Christ] says" @0323D and
   "lest [the Lord] be entreated on his behalf" @0325A. In both, Migne's Latin fixes the subject
   morphologically and English word order does not; without the bracket the English binds the
   clause to the wrong person, which is mechanism 5 committed by us rather than found.

### a.5 What I did NOT report

Column-band arithmetic (my anchors run `0310D … 0314B`, `0314C … 0317C`, `0317D … 0321B`,
`0321C … 0322A`, `0322B … 0325C`, all monotone, no text broken across an anchor); `Ibid.`
resolution; `noteCount`; Pattern 4 tails (this is lemma-and-gloss, not a florilegium); Pattern 9
items — of which my range has four and they are in §(e) as oddities only, not as defects.
No `<LACUNA>`, no Migne `( sic )`, no bracketed `Forte` occurs in my Latin.

## (b) THE SPAN COUNT, RUN AGAINST THE BRIEF'S 191 — AND IT AGREES

`data/briefs/9000-lemmata-0026.txt` declares **191 spans** for chunks 0026–0030, bands
0310C–0325C. I counted the `*…*` spans in my own five Latin files:

| chunk | italic spans in the Latin |
|---|---|
| 0026 | 45 |
| 0027 | 40 |
| 0028 | 46 |
| 0029 | 14 |
| 0030 | 46 |
| **total** | **191** |

**45 + 40 + 46 + 14 + 46 = 191. The totals agree.** The file's first entry (`[0310C] VERS. 4.--
*Qui ex vobis,*`) is the first span of chunk 0026 and its last (`[0325C] *Et omnis plebs.*`) is
the last span of chunk 0030, so the cut is aligned at both ends and no span has slid into or out
of a neighbouring stint's file. **The count was run; this is what it gave.**

Column marks and notes were counted the same way and match the Latin twin **verbatim, in order,
in place** — 15/13/15/3/14 anchors and 4/5/5/3/6 notes, checked mechanically against the Latin
files, not by eye.

## (c) THE LEMMA DIVERGENCE LIST — all 191 spans walked (7a″)

Every span was read word by word against `sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt` **at the verse
actually being glossed**, which is the thing the pre-scan's ✓ does not do. Verdict vocabulary:

- **agrees** — matches the glossed verse, allowing only Migne's orthography (*Loth*, *-cunque*).
- **clip** — cut at one or both ends of the quotation, nothing else. Declined, class (i).
- **declined** — a divergence inside the quotation, with the class and the reason given.
- **⭐ VAR** — `[var:]` fired.
- **formula** — the abridgment's `*usque ad*` splice, not scripture.

⚑ **Six of the ✓ marks in my brief file are false ✓** in the documented sense — the span occurs
somewhere in the Clementine but not at its own verse: spans 1, 4, 32, 44, 49 and, most
consequentially, **span 132**, where the pre-scan's ✓ on *In illa die* conceals the one clean
`[var:]` in chunks 0026–0028.

## (d) THIS RANGE'S CRUCES, BY COLUMN

### CHUNK 0026 — cols 0310D–0314B

**0311B · `hic majoris misericordiae et majoris gaudii` — MECHANISM 5, not resolved, rendered
adverbially.** The sentence is *majus videtur impios justificare quam justos creare et conservare,
quia etsi utrumque aequalis potentiae, **hic** majoris misericordiae et majoris gaudii*. Read as a
demonstrative pronoun, *hic* at its ordinary value is the LATTER of the two — creating and
preserving the just — which reverses the clause the sentence has just made (*majus videtur impios
justificare*). Read as the adverb, it commits to nothing. **I rendered it adverbially: "here there
is greater mercy and greater joy."** Named because the contextually easy English ("this is of
greater mercy", meaning the justification of the wicked) is a construal the printed pronoun does
not license, and it is the reading a later reader will want to substitute.

**0311C · `Quod ubi inter homines claruit, domum evertit` — MECHANISM 5, not resolved.** Two
candidate subjects for *evertit*: *Quod* (= *verbum in carne*, and the subject of *claruit*), and
*Dei sapientia*, the main-clause subject two clauses back — who is also the woman of Lc 15:8, the
one who lights the lamp and sweeps the house. **There is no morphological discriminator**:
*quod* is neuter nominative, and *evertit* carries no gender. Rendered "And when this shone forth
among men, it overturned the house", which leaves both open exactly as the Latin does.

**0311C · `evertit` for the Vulgate's `everrit`.** Lc 15:8 has *everrit domum*, "sweeps the
house"; the gloss's own word is *evertit*, "overturns". ⚑ **Almost certainly not a defect**: the
explanation attached to it is *quia conscientias hominum de reatu suo perturbavit*, which wants
overturning and not sweeping. It is unitalicised gloss prose, not a lemma, so 7a″ does not reach
it. Logged only because a mechanical collation will flag it.

**0311D · `Qui nec post culpam a Creatore periit` — MECHANISM 5, resolved.** *Qui* is masculine
singular and there are two masculine candidates in the preceding clause, *decimus electorum
numerus* and *homo*. Bound to *numerus*: it is the nearer, and the reason clause in the same
sentence (*eum sapientia Dei … ex lumine testae reparavit*) belongs to the drachma-and-number
image, not to the man. Rendered "And that number did not perish from its Creator".

**0312A · `a quo negatur`.** Declined oddity 1; see §(e). Rendered "by whom he is denied".

**0312A · `naturalibus solum ingenii … beneficium` — a case with no host.** Declined oddity 3.

**0312B · THE PARAGRAPH ENDS WITH NO FULL STOP, AND SO DOES OURS.** *Haec est prodiga vita, quae
amat fundere et spatiari in pompis exterioribus, relinquens eum qui sibi est interior* — Migne
prints no terminal mark. Pattern 8: never supply. The English stops where he stops. **Not a
defect to repair, and not a build error.**

**0313B · `munite`.** Declined oddity 2. Rendered as the imperative it is — the two purpose
clauses that follow (*ut et opus ornetur … et cursus … praeparando*) construe with it.

**0313D · `litterali sensu, contentus,` — Migne's comma falls between the ablative and its
adjective.** Carried, mark for mark: "in the literal sense, content, working earthly things,
awaiting earthly things." Pattern 8 forbids deleting a printed mark, and this is the class of
comma that gets tidied away without trace.

**0314A–0314B · convention 4 audited.** Every *gentes*-family word in this chunk stands in a
Judaei/gentes opposition — *cur Deus laetetur in assumptione **gentium*** against Israel outside
the door; *quia Jesus cum **gentibus** epularetur*; *de **gentium** salute*; *cum plenitudo
**gentium** introierit*; *Sic de Judaeo et **gentili***; *superstitiones **gentilium*** against
*connubio verbi Dei*. All are **Gentiles**. ⚑ **`natio` does not occur anywhere in chunks
0026–0030** (0 instances), `ethnicus` does not occur, and no *gentes* in my range is generic —
so nothing in this range is flattened by the convention and nothing was at risk.

### CHUNK 0027 — cols 0314C–0317C

**0314D · `Et laudavit Dominus` — Migne's capital, and the gloss reads it.** Lc 16:8's *dominus*
is the steward's master. Migne prints *Dominus*, and the gloss immediately builds on that
reading: *Si dispensator iniqui mammonae **Domini voce** laudatur*. The English follows Migne and
reads "the Lord". No marker: a capitalisation is not a textual variant, and firing on one would
be a claim about compositor's practice.

**0315B · `Deum amore non posse` — the sentence has no infinitive.** Declined oddity 6. Rendered
"he says that the lovers of money are not able God with love", which is not English and is meant
not to be: 7a says that where the printed reading will not construe, render it as closely as the
words allow and log the crux rather than substituting a word that works. **The hole is left
visible on the page.**

**0315D · `recapitulatur` singular against plural `omnia`.** Declined oddity 8. ⚑ English
"are recapitulated" has no exponent for the disagreement, so the rendering does conceal it;
Pattern 12 would mark it and this brief forbids the marker, so it is recorded here instead.

**0316A · `sicut Dominus irritus est a Pharisaeis`.** Declined oddity 7. Rendered "as the Lord is
made void by the Pharisees".

**0316A · `de terra genitu`.** Construed as an ablative of respect, "by birth from earth", which
is what the printed form allows. No conjecture offered and none needed.

**0316B · `quia praecepta pretio redimere noluit`.** Declined oddity 9.

**0316B · `gentilibus` — a fourth word on the convention-4 axis.** *Non enim gentilibus
Christianae copulantur.* The sense here is "pagans", not the typological Gentiles, and *gentilis*
is to *gentes* what *ethnicus* is (brief §1.4). I rendered **Gentiles**, for consistency with the
seven other sites in my range, and flag it so the merge can see the class rather than discover
it as drift.

**0316D · ⭐ `injusti in requie` IS NOT A DEFECT, AND IT READS AS ONE.** The Gregory extract
prints *Credendum est, quod ante retributionem extremi judicii **injusti in requie**, etc.* — as
it stands, that the unjust are at rest before the judgment, which contradicts the whole column.
It is not a dropped negative and it is not broken type: **Gregory's sentence continues *injusti
in requie **justos conspiciant***, and the abridgment's *etc.* cuts the clause before its
object.** The fragment is a truncation artifact of the recension's own splicing. Rendered as the
fragment it is ("the unjust in rest, etc."). **No marker, no conjecture, and no plate read is
owed on this column for this reason.** Recorded because a blind reader will otherwise find it
and file it as a polarity defect.

**0316D–0317A · `A longe vident infideles` — MECHANISM 5, resolved, and the easy reading is the
wrong one.** *infideles* and *fideles* are both nominative-equals-accusative plural, so the
morphology does not decide the subject. The next clause does: *fideles super se in requie
attendunt* puts the faithful above and at rest, so it is the **unfaithful** who see and the
faithful who are seen. The tempting English — "the faithful see the unfaithful afar off" — would
have inverted heaven and hell in a sentence about the day of judgment.

**0317A · `In sinum Abrahae … in quo … recipiuntur` — MECHANISM 5, named, not decisive.** *quo*
may be *sinus* (masculine) or *regnum* (neuter); both are available and the sentence names both.
Rendered so that English "which" follows "the kingdom of heaven", the nearer candidate and the
idiomatic one. I do not claim it is settled.

**0317C · `Redemptoris praecepta qui ex mortuis surrexit` — MECHANISM 5, resolved by number.**
*surrexit* is singular, so *qui* is the Redeemer and not the precepts, despite standing next to
them. Rendered "the precepts of the Redeemer, who rose from the dead".

### CHUNK 0028 — cols 0317D–0321B

**0317D · `non poterant non evenire` — a double negative, carried in full.** "which were not able
not to come about in a world most full of hardships and errors." 7a: every particle the Latin
prints appears in the English. The idiomatic English ("could not but happen") swallows one of
them and I did not use it.

**0317D · `mortem moereri perpetuam`.** Declined oddity 10.

**0318B · `quae … est eradicata et … transplantata` — MECHANISM 5, and the gender forbids the easy
reading.** The sentence is *Evangelium crucis exprimitur, **quae** per fidem apostolorum de gente
Judaeorum … est eradicata et in mare gentium transplantata*. **`quae` is FEMININE SINGULAR and
therefore cannot be *Evangelium*, which is neuter.** The available antecedents are *crucis* and
the mulberry (*morus*, feminine — and the tree is what Lc 17:6 orders uprooted and transplanted
into the sea). Rendered with a bare "which", which in English attaches to the whole phrase "the
Gospel of the cross" and so preserves the Latin's own openness without asserting the binding the
gender rules out.

**0318B · `sic diabolus, flore angelicae naturae et potestate rutilantis praevaricando terror
inhorruit odore` — will not construe.** Declined oddity 11. *rutilantis* is a genitive participle
with no host, and *terror* a nominative in a clause that already has a subject. Rendered as
closely as the words allow and left visibly broken; **no single word is nominable as the defect**,
so nothing is offered as a conjecture.

**0318D · `nec gratiam habet` — MECHANISM 5 / idiom, and I got it wrong first.** The phrase is
Lc 17:9's own — *Numquid gratiam habet servo illi* — where *gratiam habere* means to **thank**
someone. A first draft read "and has no thanks for it", which makes the master the ungratified
party and turns the a-fortiori on its head. **Corrected before this file was written, to "and
gives him no thanks."** Recorded because a crux that records a decision the text does not carry
is the failure class John @0409B is named for, and the only defence is saying which way the edit
ran.

**0319B · `unus … remanent`.** Declined oddity 12.

**0319D · `Fides quae ad agendas gratias inclinavit, econtra perfidia illos damnat` — the first
limb has no main verb.** Migne's sentence is an anacoluthon. Rendered as the fragment it is:
"The faith which inclined him to give thanks; on the contrary, faithlessness condemns those who
are ungrateful for benefits received." No repair, no supplied verb.

**0320B · `Regnum Dei intra vos est, qui … regnat` — MECHANISM 5, resolved by gender.** *qui* is
masculine and *regnum* is neuter, so the subject of *regnat* is **the Lord**, not the kingdom.
The English keeps a singular "reigns" behind a plural "you", which is what excludes the second
easy binding (to the addressees) that English word order would otherwise invite.

**0320B · `cum haeretici turbant Ecclesiam` is the VERB *turbare*, not the noun *turba*.**
Rendered "trouble the Church". Named because the brief warns that a `\bturba` grep matches this
and manufactured a false drift signal at John's merge. My range's two real *turba* nouns are at
0324B (*turba major pauperum*) and 0325B (*phantasmatum turbas*); both are **crowd/crowds**.

**0320D · `et quem fulguri … comparaverat` — MECHANISM 5, and I got this one wrong first too.**
*quem* is masculine accusative and its antecedent is *diem* — *Subitum adventus sui **diem**
pluribus astruit exemplis, et **quem** fulguri … comparaverat, diebus Noe … aequiparat*. A first
draft read "and him whom he had compared to lightning", which made the compared term the Son
rather than the day and then equated a person with the days of Noe. **Corrected to "and that
which he had compared to lightning".**

**0321A · ⭐ `cum in his successionis, in illis naturae posita sint subsidia` — MECHANISM 5,
UNRESOLVED, and the most interesting site in my range.** The sentence is *Non connubia vel
alimenta damnantur, cum in **his** successionis, in **illis** naturae posita sint subsidia*. The
argument plainly wants **marriage → succession** and **food → nature**. But taken at their
ordinary values *hic* is the LATTER (*alimenta*) and *ille* the FORMER (*connubia*), which pairs
food with succession and marriage with nature — the reverse. **English "these"/"those" reproduce
exactly the same mapping and exactly the same problem**, so I rendered them straight and left the
difficulty where Migne left it. ⛔ **Do not "fix" this by exchanging the demonstratives.** That is
the silent repair 7a exists to forbid: it would produce smooth prose, leave no lexical trace, and
pass every check we have.

**0321A · `fideres`.** Declined oddity 13 — a **non-word**, carried in italics and unmarked, and
the first of my two span deltas (see §a.4).

### CHUNK 0029 — cols 0321C–0322A

**0321C · ⭐ `[var:]` FIRED at VERS. 31, `In illa die` for Lc 17:31's `In illa hora`.** See span
132. This is the one marker in my range that the pre-scan's ✓ actively hid.

**0321D · `statum vitae qua imbutus est` — a feminine relative on a masculine noun.** Declined
oddity 14; Pattern 9, no English exponent, no emendation to make or undo.

**0322A · `quae tamen in unum molunt` — MECHANISM 5, and there is NO morphological discriminator
at all.** *Duae differentiae … Quae femineo genere designantur … ut feminae a viris, **quae** tamen
in unum molunt.* Both candidates — *differentiae* and *feminae* — are feminine plural. Decided by
the parable: the two who grind together in Lc 17:35 are the two, i.e. the differences, not the
women of the simile, and the qualification that follows (*inquantum de suis negotiis Ecclesiae
usibus deserviunt*) describes the two classes. The English keeps "which" parallel to the earlier
"Which are designated by the feminine gender", carrying the same subject forward. **Named because
it is decided on sense alone, which is the ground this brief distrusts, and a blind reader may
reasonably disagree.**

**0322A · ⭐ `[var:]` FIRED at `qui habent tanquam non habentes`.** See span 142.

**0322A · `Ubicunque fuerit Dominus corporaliter` — the gloss substitutes `Dominus` for Lc 17:37's
`corpus`.** It is **unitalicised gloss prose**, not a lemma, so it is outside the inventory and
outside 7a″; rendered as printed. Logged so that a later collation does not read the substitution
as ours.

### CHUNK 0030 — cols 0322B–0325C

**0322B · `quam nunc contra bonos quandiu hoc ipsum bonis expedit vel temporaliter aliquid
possunt` — a knotted period.** The *quandiu* clause is interpolated between the relative and its
verb. Rendered as closely as the words allow, with the interpolation kept where Migne has it
rather than moved to the end where English would prefer it. No emendation.

**0323A · `unum foramen per quos hostes intrent`.** Declined oddity 15; Pattern 9.

**0323A · `Deus non faciet vindictam electorum ad se clamantium` — unitalicised, therefore outside
the inventory.** Lc 18:7 reads *Deus autem non faciet vindictam electorum **suorum** clamantium ad
se **die ac nocte***. Migne's gloss compresses it and does not present it as a quotation
(no italics), so no `[var:]` is owed and none is fired. Logged for completeness.

**0323D · `et non Deum vel Dei Filium confessus est dicit` — a subject change across a gap Migne
leaves unmarked.** *confessus est* is the ruler; *dicit* is **Christ**, settled by the next
sentence (*Christus autem non renuit testimonium bonitatis, sed magistri absque Deo excludit
bonitatem*). An unmarked "he says" in English binds to the ruler and would have the ruler assert
that a holy man is not good — the opposite of what he has just failed to confess. **The English
supplies "[Christ]" in square brackets**, our word and visibly ours. Migne's missing punctuation
between the two verbs is carried as printed.

**0324A · `contra naturam esse dives misericors` — nominatives inside an accusative-and-
infinitive.** Declined oddity 16. English has no exponent, so the rendering ("a merciful rich man
is against nature") conceals it; recorded here instead.

**0324B–0325B · conventions 2 and 5 audited.** *turba* → **crowd** (0324B, 0325B); **`multitudo`
does not occur in my range at all**, so the pair cannot collapse here. *daemonium* → **demon**
(0312A *Cibus daemonum*, 0314B *cum daemone*); *diabolus* → **devil** (0312B, 0312C, 0322A);
**`satanas` does not occur.** *mysterium* → **mystery** (0323C, 0324C); *sacramentum* →
**sacrament** (0313C ×2, 0315D). ⚑ Gloss-voice archaism: *dicit*/*ait* are **says/said**
throughout, and **every `-eth` form in my five chunks stands inside an italic span** — the
italic-membership test was run, not assumed.

**0324D · `Testatamenti`.** Declined oddity 17 — a **non-word**, carried in italics and unmarked,
and the second of my two span deltas.

**0325A · `homo ad cognoscenda redit`.** Declined oddity 18.

**0325A · `significant caecum carnalium vitiorum`.** Declined oddity 19 — and note that the
relative that follows is *quae*, neuter plural, binding to *vitiorum*, which is what shows that
the thing signified must be a collective and not a person.

**0325A · `ne pro se exorari possit` — MECHANISM 5 plus a voice, both preserved.** *exorari* is
**passive**, and the one entreated is the Lord: the parallel eight words earlier is *ne Jesus ad
illuminandum venire possit cor hominis*. *se* is the blind man. Rendered "lest [the Lord] be
entreated on his behalf", with the supplied subject bracketed. Making it active ("lest he obtain
by entreaty for himself") would be 7a mechanism 3 — a passive turned active, leaving no lexical
trace — and it is the rendering the sentence invites.

**0325B · `Respice, fides tua salvum te fecit`.** See span 189; a plate candidate, not a marker.

### ⚑ THE ONE CONVENTION EXCEPTION FORCED BY LUKE — 0316A, and the Latin that forced it

Convention 6 fixes the abridgment's splice formula *usque ad* as **"as far as"**. My range has
**six** occurrences of the formula proper — 0313A (AMBR.), 0315B (AUG.), 0316B (GREG.), 0316D
(GREG.), 0323D (BEDA.), 0324A (AMBR.) — each italic and each following `etc.,`. All six are
rendered **"as far as"**. Two further, non-formula, unitalicised occurrences in ordinary prose
are also rendered "as far as" and read naturally: *in verbo **usque ad** peregrinationem nostram
condescendens* (0313A) and *ire crescendo **usque ad** perfectiora* (0319D).

**The exception is span 66, at 0316A:**

> *Quamvis dicam,* **Lex et prophetae usque ad Joannem,** *tamen non praedico legis
> destructionem.*

This *usque ad* is **Luke 16:16 itself** — an italic scripture lemma quoted inside the gloss's
own sentence, not a splice mark, and the very verse the chunk's VERS. 16 address (span 63, *Lex
et prophetae.*) heads. Rendered **"The law and the prophets until John."** Applying convention
6 here would put the splice formula's English inside a lemma where nothing is spliced, which is
the opposite of what the convention exists to mark. Mark's cruces record two such look-alikes on
that book and decided them per occurrence; this is the same decision, and it is stated here so
that a mechanical sweep does not "normalise" it back.

---

## (e) DECLINED-ODDITY LIST — NINETEEN SUSPECT WORDS, NONE MARKED, ALL FOR THE PLATE

**No `[sic:]` was fired anywhere in this range.** Every entry below is rendered as printed (or
carried in italics where it is not a word at all), and for every one of them **I do not know
whose the defect is and I do not claim to.** Where an internal control exists I name it and say
in the same breath that a control proves an error exists and never proves who made it.

Ordered by column, which is the order a plate reader will work in.
Leaf calibration for this range, from the brief: `patrologiaecursu0114mign`, **leaf n = (column +
11) / 2** → cols 0310–0325 sit on leaves **n160–n168**.

| # | col | printed | class | conjecture, and the control if any |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **0312A** | *a quo **negatur*** | real word, wrong mood and wrong sense | *regatur*. Indicative among the subjunctive *formetur*, and the same sentence closes *ut nullo formante, nullo **regente** vivat* — the *formare/regere* pair is on the page. ⚑ Two letters; a plausible type-batter and a plausible OCR read alike. Rendered "by whom he is denied". |
| 2 | **0312A** | *naturalibus solum ingenii … beneficium* | a case with no host | *naturale solum ingenii beneficium*. Ablative plural beside a genitive and an accusative. Rendered "only the natural benefit of native wit". |
| 3 | **0312A** | *per**ae**gre profectus* | orthography or type | *peregre* (Lc 15:13 *peregre profectus est*). May be an attested spelling; listed because it is one letter from the verse it is expounding. |
| 4 | **0313B** | *exempla priorum **munite*** | real word, wrong part of speech | *munimen*. A 2nd-plural imperative in a string of nominal appositions (*Id est signaculum fidei … Id est operatione … Id est exempla priorum munite*). It does take an English slot as an imperative and the two purpose clauses construe with it, so it is **rendered**, not carried. |
| 5 | **0314B** | ***Filii**, tu semper* | one letter | *Fili* (Lc 15:31, vocative). Rendered "Son". A doubled *i* is also a common medieval orthography, which is exactly why this needs a plate and not a guess. |
| 6 | **0315B** | *Deum **amore** non posse* | real word, **no grammatical slot** | *amare*. *posse* is left with no infinitive and the clause will not construe. ⚑ **Control, and it settles nothing:** the same sentence prints *qui ergo Deum **amat*** nine words later and *transitoria et aeterna **diligere*** sixteen words later. Rendered, unconstruably, "are not able God with love" — the hole is left on the page rather than papered over. |
| 7 | **0316A** | *Dominus **irritus** est a Pharisaeis* | real word, wrong sense | *irrisus*. The clause it closes is about *linguas **deridentium***, mockers, and *irritus* means "void". Rendered "is made void". Pattern 18 shape; declined because the English is odd rather than false. |
| 8 | **0315D** | *omnia … **recapitulatur*** | number disagreement | singular verb, plural subject. ⚑ **English has no exponent**, so "are recapitulated" silently repairs it. Pattern 12(2) would mark it; this brief forbids the marker; it is therefore recorded only here. |
| 9 | **0316B** | *quia **praecepta** pretio redimere noluit* | real word, wrong sense | *peccata*. In a Gregory extract on the rich man's want of pity. ⚑ **Control, and it settles nothing:** 0315C, in this same stint's range, prints *sua quae forte inciderent **peccata**, eleemosynis redimere*. Rendered "to redeem the precepts with a price". |
| 10 | **0317D** | *mortem **moereri** perpetuam* | orthography or wrong verb | *mereri*. |
| 11 | **0318B** | *flore angelicae naturae et potestate **rutilantis** praevaricando **terror** inhorruit **odore*** | a clause that will not construe | **none offered.** *rutilantis* is a genitive participle with no host and *terror* a nominative in a clause that already has a subject. No single word is nominable as the defect, so nothing is proposed. Rendered as closely as the words allow. |
| 12 | **0319B** | *Sed **unus** gratias agit, id est qui … **remanent*** | number disagreement | singular subject, plural verb. Rendered with the plural ("they who remain"), which is the reading the following *novem sunt* requires. |
| 13 | **0321A** | ***fideres*** | ⛔ **NON-WORD** | *fideles* — *cum Dominus fideres in Ecclesia congregat*. **Carried untranslated in italics per Pattern 7/10 and NOT marked, per this brief.** ⚑ l→r is a classic transcription confusion AND a classic worn-sort; 9001 @0203A *magnitudino* is the standing reminder that a non-word is not automatically ours. **This is span delta 1: chunk 0028 ships 47 English italic spans against the Latin's 46.** |
| 14 | **0321D** | *statum vitae **qua** imbutus est* | gender mismatch | *quo*. Pattern 9: no English exponent, nothing to emend either way. |
| 15 | **0323A** | *unum foramen **per quos** hostes intrent* | number and gender mismatch | *per quod*. Pattern 9. |
| 16 | **0324A** | *contra naturam esse **dives misericors*** | case mismatch | *divitem misericordem*. Nominatives inside an accusative-and-infinitive; English has no exponent and the rendering conceals it. |
| 17 | **0324D** | ***Testatamenti*** | ⛔ **NON-WORD** | *Testamenti* — *alium Veteris, alium Novi esse Deum Testatamenti*. ⚑ **A doubled syllable, which is the shape of the brief's orphan-syllable class** — 8999 @0361A, where Corpus Corporum resolved a hyphenation *and kept the orphan as well*, so our file printed the syllable twice. If Migne broke *Testa-/menti* at a line turn, this is that class exactly; if he did not, it is his compositor. **I cannot tell from our file and I do not guess.** Carried in italics, unmarked. **This is span delta 2: chunk 0030 ships 47 English italic spans against the Latin's 46.** |
| 18 | **0325A** | *homo **ad cognoscenda** redit* | a gerundive with no noun | none determinate. Rendered "man returns to the things that are to be known". |
| 19 | **0325A** | *significant **caecum** carnalium vitiorum* | real word, right case, **nonsense in place** | *coetum* / *cuneum* / *turbam*. The relative that follows is *quae*, neuter plural, binding to *vitiorum*, so what is signified must be a **collective**, not a person — and the chapter's actual blind man is the one being kept from Jesus by these, not identified with them. Rendered "the blind man of carnal vices". ⚑ Pattern 18 shape and the strongest of the four I declined; declined because *caecum* in a chapter about a *caecus* is precisely the kind of internal corroboration the brief measures as uncorrelated with truth. |

### Two further plate candidates that are not single suspect words

- **0325B** *Respice, fides tua **salvum te** fecit* against Lc 18:42 *te salvum fecit*, with the
  received order printed at **0319D** in the same stint's range as the on-page control (spans 189
  and 111). A word-order transposition, unmarkable because our English has no exponent for it.
- **0310D** *Imponit in **humerum*** against Lc 15:5 *in humeros suos*, with the gloss's own
  *humeris* and *humeri* immediately after it (span 4).

# STINT 0031 — chunks 0031–0035, cols 0325C–0340C

*Preserved verbatim from `cruces-0031.md`. Where it states a `[var:]` threshold of its own, §0.1 governs.*

# Cruces — 9000, *Glossa ordinaria*, **Evangelium secundum Lucam** — stint 0031 (chunks 0031–0035, cols 0325C–0340C)

Capita XIX–XXII: the entry into Jerusalem, the temple disputes, the apocalyptic discourse,
and the opening of the passion. 5,933 Latin words, 186 italic spans, 23 notes, 60 column
anchors.

---

## (a) THE POLICY I APPLIED — stated before the findings, so it can be argued with

**1. No `[sic:]`. Not one, on any word, however good the internal argument.** The launch
brief's numbers govern: on John the 23 leaf-reads spent on *fired* markers produced zero
corrections, and the nineteen *declined* oddities produced all seventeen. My attribution guess
carries no evidential weight, and the brief's within-work table (cols 381–390 four of four
Migne's; cols 399–415 five of five ours, ~200 columns apart in one book) says no prior may be
inherited from a neighbouring range either. **Every suspect word in this range is in §(e),
named, unattributed, with the leaf that would settle it.** Where a defect could be Migne's type
or Corpus Corporum's transcription I name both and assert neither.

**2. No `[cj:]` either.** Pattern 18's bar is that the printed word must make the sentence say
something the author is arguing against, and 18a scopes the extension to negations and
correlatives. Three sites in my range came close and all three are declined in §(e), with the
reason: **@0332A *utile cadaver*** (a coherent reading exists — the corpse they discarded was
in fact of use, and the Gentiles took it up, which is the chapter's whole argument),
**@0335A *illuc* for *illud*** (a pronoun slip, not a negation), and **@0338D *aut ne … aut***
(a genuine correlative disturbance, but I cannot name the true reading without the plate, and
"where a reading is merely uncertain, it stays in the crux").

**3. `[var:]` only for a divergent quoted CLAUSE I can cite** against
`sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt` by book chapter:verse. **Five fired.** I declined every
one-word apparent divergence — *aut*/*vel*, *enim* added, *converte*/*convertere*,
*resurgent*/*resurgant*, *intraverit*/*intraret*, *non*/*nondum*, dropped *ergo* — and every
pure word-order or truncation case, following the peer stint that got this right this run.
A single word is suspicion of our file; a semantically coherent divergent clause is evidence
about Migne.

**4. One `[d:]` fired**, at @0326B (*id est id est*), under Pattern 11: an unmarked repetition
would read as *our* build defect. ⚠ **I do not know whose doubling it is** — the marker's
tooltip says Migne's plate, and I record here that the plate has not been read. It is also in
§(e) as an oddity so the reader can settle it at leaf 168.

**5. Inherited conventions §1 applied from the first chunk, not reconciled afterwards.**
Measured across my whole range, English against Latin:

| axis | Latin (chunks 31–35) | English | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| *turba* (noun only) | 6 | crowd 6 | **exact 1:1** |
| *multitudo* | 2 | multitude 2 | **exact 1:1** |
| *daemonium* | 0 | demon 0 | vacuous, nothing to flatten |
| *diabolus* / *Satanas* | 8 | devil 3 + Satan 5 | **exact 1:1** |
| *mysterium* | 5 | mystery 1 + mysteries 4 | **exact 1:1** |
| *sacramentum* | 9 | sacrament 3 + sacraments 6 | **exact 1:1** |
| *natio* | 3 (*nationum*) | "nations" 3 | **1:1** |
| *gens* sing. of the Jewish people | 2 (*gente*) | "their own nation", "that whole nation" | per convention 4 |
| *gentes* plural | 19 | Gentiles 18 + nations 1 | see note below |
| italic *usque ad* | 3 | **as far as** 3 | **exact 1:1**, zero "down to" |
| gloss-voice archaism | — | *-eth* 3 | all three (*cometh*, *receiveth*, *betrayeth*) inside italic scripture spans; zero on a metalanguage verb |
| em-dashes | 0 | **0** | — |

⚑ **The one *gentes* rendered "nations"** is @0335B *populo illi per omnes gentes disperso* —
the plainly generic dispersion sense, which convention 4 permits. All eighteen others sit in
the *Judaei*/*gentes* typology that runs through capita XIX–XX and take **Gentiles**.

⚑ **`-est` second-person forms** (*knewest*, *reapest*, *givest*, *wishest*, *mayest*) are
**Pattern 17**, not archaism drift: each renders a Latin second-person **singular** in the
gloss's own voice (*noveras*, *metis*, *das*, *vis*, *resurgas*), and Pattern 17 makes *thou*
a number marker, not a reverence marker. They are not the class convention 1 forbids.

⚑ **Both instrument warnings in the brief fired in my range and were caught.** A bare
`\bturba` grep here matches *turbatam* (@0335C, *turbata faciem velant*) and *turbatis*
(@0333D, *conscientia turbatis*) — both the verb *turbare*, neither a crowd. A bare `natio`
grep matches *damnationem* three times and *damnationis* once. My table above counts the noun
forms only.

**6. Negation fidelity (§4 / 7a).** Every `non`, `nec`, `neque`, `nisi`, `nullus`, `nihil`,
`nequaquam`, `necdum` the Latin prints stands in the English, including where carrying it
leaves the sentence odd. Two notes: @0340A *nonnunquam* is one lexeme meaning "sometimes" and
is rendered so — flagged here to pre-empt a particle count reading it as a lost negation; and
@0332D *Non enim potest quis esse Domini, nisi…* is rendered "no one can be the Lord's, unless
he has first renounced the world", which is the English exponent of *non … quis*, not a
dropped particle.

**7. Pattern 7 / Pattern 8 literalism, three visible costs, all deliberate.** @0327B carries
Migne's missing full stop (*legis dicta Mna decem drachmas appendit*), @0338C carries a second
(*poenitentiam agat praedixit et poenam*), and @0338C ends a paragraph with no stop at all
(*et criminosa*). Supplying the stops would have been a silent repair of exactly the class 9001
@0202D was nearly published with. @0329D carries a singular verb against a plural relative
(*humiliatur qui … indigent*) rather than smoothing it to "they are humbled".

## (b) SPAN COUNT AGAINST THE BRIEF — I RAN IT, AND IT AGREES

`data/briefs/9000-lemmata-0031.txt` declares **186 spans** for chunks 0031–0035, bands
0325C–0339C.

I counted the paired `*…*` runs in the body of my own five Latin files (frontmatter excluded):

| file | spans |
|---|---|
| `src/latin/9000/0031.md` | 57 |
| `src/latin/9000/0032.md` | 16 |
| `src/latin/9000/0033.md` | 44 |
| `src/latin/9000/0034.md` | 29 |
| `src/latin/9000/0035.md` | 40 |
| **total** | **186** |

**186 = 186. No head-of-range slide, no shortfall.** The brief file's own inventory lines also
number 186, so the three counts agree. (Cross-check on the English side: each chunk's paired
runs equal its Latin's plus exactly the italic Latin I put inside my own `[var:]` markers —
0031 57+5=62, 0032 16+2=18, 0033 44+1=45, 0034 29+0=29, 0035 40+0=40.)

Marker parity, counted before writing each file and again after: **column anchors
16 / 5 / 13 / 10 / 16 — verbatim, in order, in place; notes 8 / 0 / 7 / 3 / 5 — matching each
frontmatter's `noteCount`, contents untranslated.** `node scripts/verify-english.mjs 9000`
reports **verify OK**.

## (c) THE LEMMA DIVERGENCE LIST — all 186 spans, in order

**Five `[var:]` fired** (rows 11, 14, 23, 65, 83). Every other divergence I saw is listed with
the reason I declined it. The pre-scan column reproduces the brief's mark; ✓ is not clearance
and ⚠ is not a divergence, and I walked every line either way.

| # | col | span | pre-scan | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | [0325C] | *Et ingressus,* | ✓ | Walked word by word against Lc/the cited verse; agrees with the printed Latin. No divergence. |
| 2 | [0325D] | *justificatus,* | single | Single word; walked in place against the verse being glossed. No divergence. |
| 3 | [0325D] | *Et ipse dives.* | ✓ | Walked word by word against Lc/the cited verse; agrees with the printed Latin. No divergence. |
| 4 | [0325D] | VERS. 3.-- *Prae turba.* | ✓ | Walked word by word against Lc/the cited verse; agrees with the printed Latin. No divergence. |
| 5 | [0325D] | VERS. 4.-- *Sycomorum.* | single | Single word; walked in place against the verse being glossed. No divergence. |
| 6 | [0326A] | *ficus fatua,* | ⚠ | Not scripture: Jerome's etymology of *sycomorus*. No Vulgate comparand exists. No marker. |
| 7 | [0326A] | *Quia inde erat transiturus. [n: (ISID.)]* | ✓ | Walked word by word against Lc/the cited verse; agrees with the printed Latin. No divergence. |
| 8 | [0326A] | *usque ad* | ✓ | The abridgment's splice formula, not a quotation. Rendered **as far as** (inherited convention 6). |
| 9 | [0326A] | VERS. 5.-- *Et cum venisset.* | ✓ | Walked word by word against Lc/the cited verse; agrees with the printed Latin. No divergence. |
| 10 | [0326B] | *Descende.* | single | Single word; walked in place against the verse being glossed. No divergence. |
| 11 | [0326B] | *Etsi cognovimus secundum carnem Christum, sed jam non novimus. Si enim mortuus est ex infirmitate, sed jam vivit ex virtute Dei [n: (II Cor. V, XIII)]* | ⚠ | **`[var:]` FIRED.** A conflation of 2 Cor 5:16 + 13:4, and Migne's own note says so. Divergence marked: 13:4 reads *crucifixus est ex infirmitate*, Migne *mortuus est*; a lexical substitution in a quoted clause, and the gloss is expounding the cross (the sycomore). The 5:16 half also drops *nunc* — recorded inside the same marker, not separately marked. |

**Summary of (c): 186 spans walked.**

- **5 `[var:]` fired** — rows 11, 14, 23, 65, 83.
- **15 real divergences seen and DECLINED** — rows 22, 29, 34, 69, 80, 87, 88, 94, 109, 126,
  136, 145, 171, 181, 184. Every one is either a single word (*aut*/*vel*, added *enim*,
  *converte*/*convertere*, *resurgent*/*resurgant*, *intraverit*/*intraret*, *non*/*nondum*,
  dropped *ergo*, dropped *nunc*) or pure word order, or a truncation. Reasons are in the rows.
- **5 that are NOT divergences though the pre-scan flagged them** — rows 53, 117, 153 and 154
  (lemma addresses shortened or recast to head a gloss, the commonest shape in this recension)
  and row 120 (*caeteri* for *ceteri*, orthography, Pattern 9).
- **3 that are not scripture at all** — rows 6, 48, 49: Jerome's etymologies of *sycomorus*,
  Bethphage and Bethania, which have no Vulgate comparand.
- **3 abridgment splice formulas** — rows 8, 167, 168: the italic *usque ad*, all rendered
  **as far as**.

⚑ **One false ✓ found, of the class the brief warns about.** Row 65 (*Gloria in excelsis Deo*)
scored **✓ Clementine verbatim** and is one of the five markers I fired: the sequence does occur
in the Clementine — at **Luke 19:38** — but not at Luke 2:14, which is the verse being quoted.
That is precisely the failure mode the brief names ("it does not mean it occurs at the verse
being glossed"), and it is the most interesting divergence in my range. **A ✓ is not clearance,
and here it was actively misleading.**

**Four wrong chapter references** noticed and, per refDisplay convention, neither corrected nor
queried: *Act. IX* for Acts 11 (row 14), *Psal. CXVII* for Ps 2 (row 90), *Phil. III* for Phil 2
(row 173), *Luc. XII* for Luke 22 (row 149). *Psal. LXXVII* for Ps 79 (row 88) is the Greek
numbering and is not an error.

⚠ **One warning about the base text, not about Migne.** At row 145 our own
`sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt` prints 1 Thess 5:3 as *tunc **repentius** eis superveniet
interitus*. The Clementine reads *repentinus*. I therefore did not cite that verse in a marker,
and the flat file is worth a look before anyone else does.

## (d) THIS RANGE'S CRUCES, BY COLUMN

### The five `[var:]`, restated with their grounds

| col | Migne prints | received text | why it is material |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0326B | *Si enim **mortuus** est ex infirmitate* | Vulg. 2 Cor 13:4 *etsi **crucifixus** est ex infirmitate* | The gloss has just made the sycomore the cross; the proof-text is adduced for the crucifixion and the word "crucified" is gone from it. Migne's own note (*II Cor. V, XIII*) shows the conflation is deliberate; the substitution inside 13:4 is not covered by it. |
| 0326B | *Quare introisti ad **gentes** praeputium habentes* | Vulg. Acts 11:3 *ad **viros** praeputium habentes* | The gloss cites it as proof that the Jews hated the Gentiles' salvation. Migne's *gentes* supplies the very word the argument needs and the Vulgate does not print. |
| 0327A | *Hosanna filio David: benedictus qui venit in nomine Domini **rex Israel*** | Matt 21:9 (*hosanna in altissimis*) + Jo 12:13 (*rex Israel*) | Matches no single verse, and matches Luke's own 19:38 (*Benedictus qui venit rex in nomine Domini*) least of all — in a gloss on Luke 19. |
| 0330A | *Gloria in **excelsis** Deo* | Vulg. Lc 2:14 *in **altissimis*** | 7a″ exactly: the nativity verse conformed to *in excelsis*, which is the Clementine's reading at Lc **19:38** — the verse the gloss is expounding two lines later. The parallel the gloss draws is the thing that produced the conformation. |
| 0331A | *Vinea Domini **sabaoth**, domus Israel* | Vulg. Isa 5:7 *Vinea enim Domini **exercituum** domus Israel est* | The untranslated Hebraism stands where the Vulgate renders the divine title; a lexical substitution inside a quoted clause. |

### The one `[d:]`

**@0326B `id est id est`** — *Manebat aliquando Dominus in domo principis Pharisaeorum, id est
id est in Judaeorum Synagoga docebat.* Two identical runs, so Pattern 11 puts the marker on the
second. ⚠ **Whose doubling this is, I do not know.** It has the shape of a compositor's eye-skip
and equally the shape of a transcription artifact at a line turn. Leaf 168. If the plate prints
it once, the `[d:]` comes out and the Latin gets a patch.

### Mechanism-5 census — the WHOLE range, not the first chunks

Sites where a pronoun, relative or participle could bind to either of two nouns, checked on
case and gender rather than on sense. All five chunks were censused.

| col | the ambiguity | the easier reading | what the morphology decides |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0326B | *elegit eligentem se, et amat amantem se* | the Gentile people chooses and loves | the Lord is subject throughout; *elevatum*/*eligentem*/*amantem* are all accusative objects. |
| 0328D | *quae … ipsum occisura … sit peritura* | *quae* = the parable (fem.) | *quae* is the **city** (*civitatis*, six words back), which both kills him and perishes. |
| **0329A** | *sed dominatum sibi vindicare non poterat, **quem Dominum fecerat non natura, sed culpa*** | the Lord could not claim mastery | ⭐ The subject is the **false master** the colt served by error, and *quem* is that same master — the sentence exists to say fault, not nature, made him lord. Migne capitalises *Domino*/*Dominum* here, which is exactly the pull toward the wrong noun. Rendered lower-case "lord" for the false master and capital "Lord" only at *cum Dominus dicitur*, where the true one is meant; the capitalisation itself is logged in §(e). |
| 0330C | *quam facit speluncam latronum **qui** … meditatur agere* | *qui* = the conscience | *quam* (fem. acc.) is the conscience; *qui* (masc. nom. sg.) is the man who makes it a den. |
| 0331D | *Haereditatem … **quam non moriens Pater reliquit**, sed ipse … possedit* | the *non* negates *possedit* | *non* governs *moriens … reliquit* only; the second limb is affirmative, and the point is that the Father did not die. |
| 0332B | *hanc ipsi Domino inhabitatore dignam quasi domum constituere* | *dignam* agrees with *Domino* | *dignam* is fem. acc. agreeing with *hanc* (the people-as-house); *Domino inhabitatore* is the ablative *dignus* governs. |
| 0333A | *turpitudinem fabulae fingunt, **quae** … arguit eos qui resurrectionem asserunt* | *quae* = the baseness | either construes; *fabulae* is the operative noun and the Sadducees' invented tale is what convicts. Rendered "a tale, which convicts". |
| 0333C | *unitatem naturae, **qua** in altero aliter operatur* | *qua* = *infirmitatem* | *qua* is fem. abl. agreeing with *unitatem*, the affirmed limb, not with the denied *infirmitatem*. |
| 0333D | *Non salutari in foro, nec primas sedes vetat **eis quibus debentur** … sed **eis qui indebite amant*** | the *non* negates only *salutari* | The *non* governs the whole *vetat*-clause and its datives: what he does not forbid is these things *to those to whom they are due*. Both datives carried. |
| 0334A | *illos etiam … damnationem quidem mereri, sed **istos** majorem* | *istos* = the corner-prayers | *illos* are those praying in corners; *istos* are the scribes of the lemma. Kept distinct. |
| 0334A | *Ecclesia … **quae** omne quod innuit … intelligit* | *quae* = *munera* | *quae* is nom. fem. sg. = *Ecclesia*, and the verb is singular. |
| 0335C | *appropinquante termino **suo*** | the world's end | *suo* attaches to *elementa*, the subject of *nutant et fremunt*. |
| 0337A | *quem prius intraverat ut deciperet* | *quem* = the morsel | masc. acc. = Judas; the morsel is *buccellam*, fem. |
| 0338A | *cum ipse aequanimiter flagella tulit **alienae*** | *alienae* with *flagella* | *alienae* is fem. gen. sg. agreeing with the understood *culpae* of the previous clause — another's **fault**, not another's scourges. |
| **0340A** | *ut sciant legentes non facultatem resistendi deesse discipulis, sed amorem **magistro** potius **inesse** patiendi* | the love of suffering was in the **disciples** | ⭐⭐ **The decisive site of the range.** *inesse* takes the dative and *magistro* is that dative. The momentum of the sentence hands you "the disciples" — they are the subject two words earlier — and that reading is wrong. The paragraph proves it twice over: *duo gladii sufficiunt ad testimonium **sponte passi Salvatoris***, and eight lines later *Domino etiam morituro pietatem virtutemque medicandi **inesse***, the same verb with the same dative. Rendered "but that rather the love of suffering was in the master." |
| 0340B | *Unus qui et **apostolis** audaciam certandi … et … **Domino** etiam morituro pietatem virtutemque medicandi **inesse** doceret* | one dative for both | Two distinct datives share one *inesse*: boldness in the apostles, mercy and healing in the Lord. Kept apart in the English; collapsing them would have made the apostles the healers. |

### Other cruces by column

- **@0327C `Quidam simplicioris observationis devotionem … percipient`.** *devotionem* is
  accusative with nothing to govern it (*percipient* already has *praemia*). Rendered with the
  stranded accusative visible ("Certain men, a devotion of simpler observance, will receive…")
  rather than repaired to an ablative. Declined oddity, leaf 169.
- **@0327D `ut quod exigeretur ab eo qui nihil recipit?`** Reads as though for *ut quid*
  ("why should it be exacted…"). Both are real Latin; carried as printed, with the question
  mark Migne prints. Declined oddity, leaf 169.
- **@0332A `quasi utile cadaver projicientes`.** The obvious conjecture is *inutile*, and
  "casting him out as a **useful** corpse" is the shape a `[cj:]` is written for. **I decline
  it**, and the reason is not caution: a coherent reading exists and it is the chapter's own
  argument — the corpse they threw away was of use, and they *gave him to the Gentiles to be
  taken up* in the same clause. Pattern 18's bar is that the printed word makes the sentence
  say what the author is arguing against, and here it does not. Logged, not marked. Leaf 171.
- **@0334B `ut velamento litterae occultantis, recondita sacramenta … incipiant patescere`.**
  An ablative absolute with no participle to complete it (*sublato*? *remoto*?). Rendered
  literally ("so that, with the veil of the concealing letter, the hidden sacraments … may
  begin to be disclosed") without supplying a verb. Leaf 172.
- **@0336B–C punctuation.** Migne's `Sic et nos cum … non desperemus` is an *ut*-less
  hortatory after a *cum*-clause; rendered as printed, not smoothed to an indicative.

---

## (e) DECLINED-ODDITY LIST — every suspect word I did NOT mark

**This is the list the plate reader should work from.** On John this class produced all
seventeen corrections and the fired markers produced none. **For each entry I state what I saw
and refuse to say whose it is**; where the shape names a mechanism from the brief's Addendum A
I say which. Leaf n = (column + 11) / 2, so leaf n carries columns 2n−11 and 2n−10:
**325/326 → 168 · 327/328 → 169 · 329/330 → 170 · 331/332 → 171 · 333/334 → 172 ·
335/336 → 173 · 337/338 → 174 · 339/340 → 175.**

| # | col | leaf | what is printed | what is odd about it | class |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0326B | 168 | *id est **id est** in Judaeorum Synagoga* | An exact doubling. `[d:]` fired (Pattern 11 requires it, or the repetition reads as ours), but **the attribution is open**: a compositor's eye-skip and a transcription artifact at a line turn look identical here. | dittography |
| 2 | 0326D | 168 | *propter poenitentiam peccatorum **suspiciendam*** | A real word ("to be looked up to") where *suscipiendam* ("to be taken up") is what the clause wants; the gloss is about Christ receiving penitents. One letter apart, both well-formed. | real word, misleading |
| 3 | 0327B | 169 | *ut spiritualiter intelligat legis dicta **Mna** decem drachmas appendit* | **No full stop** between *dicta* and *Mna*, where a sentence plainly ends. Carried as printed under Pattern 8. Same shape as 9001 @0202D, where the stop turned out to be the digitization's. | punctuation |
| 4 | 0327C | 169 | *Quidam simplicioris observationis **devotionem** … percipient* | Stranded accusative; *devotione* would construe. Real word, real form. | case anomaly |
| 5 | 0327D | 169 | *VERS. 18.-- Et alter, **Bonus ordo*** | No punctuation closes the italic lemma, where every parallel address in the work prints *etc.* or a stop. | punctuation |
| 6 | 0327D | 169 | *ut **quod** exigeretur ab eo qui nihil recipit?* | *ut quid* is what the interrogative wants. Both real. | real word, misleading |
| 7 | 0328A | 169 | *neque **audito Evangelio dormierunt*** | In a clause about those to whom the law was never given, "nor did they sleep when the Gospel had been heard" does not follow. Every word is well-formed; I cannot see what is wrong with it, only that something is. | unconstruable |
| 8 | 0328D | 169 | *non vult abscondi civitatem **supra se** positam* | Matt 5:14 is *civitas supra montem posita*. *supra se* is grammatical and means "set above itself". | real word, misleading |
| 9 | 0329A | 170 | *addictus vel astrictus **Domino** errore famulabatur* | Capital *D* on what the same sentence identifies as the **false** master (*quem Dominum fecerat non natura, sed culpa*). Rendered lower-case; a reader with the plate should confirm the capital is Migne's. | capitalisation |
| 10 | 0329C | 170 | *doctrina virtutum, vel discretio Scripturarum, et gratia **spiritualiter*** | An adverb in a list of three nominatives, where *spiritualis* is expected. Real word. | real word, misleading |
| 11 | 0329D | 170 | ***humiliatur** qui ejus misericordia **indigent**, ut ab eo **exaltentur*** | Singular verb, plural relative, plural purpose clause. Carried as printed ("he is humbled who need his mercy, that they may be exalted"); *humiliantur* would resolve it. Pattern 18a declines number anomalies, and so do I. | number anomaly |
| 12 | 0330D | 170 | *quia, **neglecto,** suo magisterio* | A comma between the participle and its noun inside an ablative absolute. Punctuation only; the English reads it as one phrase. | punctuation |
| 13 | 0332A | 171 | *sed quasi **utile** cadaver projicientes* | *inutile* is the obvious conjecture. **Declined deliberately** — see §(d): the printed reading is coherent and is the chapter's own argument, so Pattern 18's bar is not met. If the plate reads *inutile* the English needs one word changed. | real word, coherent |
| 14 | 0332A | 171 | ***divinae scientiae legis** … ad gentes transferri* | Genitive where *transferri* wants an accusative subject (*divinam scientiam*). | case anomaly |
| 15 | 0333B | 172 | *Quia vero **resurgent** mortui* | Vulg. Lc 20:37 *resurgant*. One letter, both real forms; declined as a single-word suspicion per Addendum A rather than fired as a `[var:]`. | one-word variant |
| 16 | 0334A | 172 | *quae omne quod **innuit**, non sui meriti … intelligit* | "all that she nods at / hints" for what the Church casts into God's gifts. Real word; *immittit* or *mittit* would suit the sentence, which is about *mittere*. | real word, misleading |
| 17 | 0334B | 172 | *ut **velamento litterae occultantis**, recondita sacramenta … incipiant patescere* | An ablative absolute with no participle. A dropped word would explain it; so would nothing at all. | apparent omission |
| 18 | 0335A | 173 | *quandoquidem nec **illuc** potest perire in vobis* | *illud* is what the relative *quod incisum non dolet* answers to. *illuc* is a real adverb, one letter away. Rendered "not even there". | real word, misleading |
| 19 | 0335C | 173 | *terrarumque orbis prementibus undique **colonis** inficitur* | "husbandmen pressing on every side" in an apocalyptic clause about the sea, the waves and the lights of heaven. Real word, wrong register. | real word, misleading |
| 20 | 0337C | 174 | *Qui in littera remanet, **intimis** Pascha facit* | The sentence's contrast is with *in sublimi loco* and *in mentis solio*, which wants *in imis* ("in the depths"). **This is the fused shape of Pattern 10 / the brief's hyphen-rejoin class** — two halves of a real phrase resolved into one plausible token — and no frequency test can tell it from Migne's own. Rendered "in the innermost places". | word division (fused) |
| 21 | 0338C | 174 | *ut conscius poenitentiam agat **praedixit** et poenam* | **No stop** between *agat* and *praedixit*, where a sentence ends. Carried. Second instance of the same shape as #3, two columns apart. | punctuation |
| 22 | 0338C | 174 | *caduca amare convincitur **et criminosa*** | The paragraph ends with **no full stop at all**. Carried; the English paragraph ends unpunctuated too. | punctuation |
| 23 | 0338D | 174 | ***aut ne** districtio rigida, **aut** pietas sit remissa* | A correlative that does not close: *ut ne … aut*, or Gregory's *nec … nec*, would. This is the nearest thing in my range to a Pattern 18 correlative site; declined because I cannot name the true reading without the plate. | correlative |
| 24 | 0337A | 174 | *et **carnis sanguinisque suis discipulis** sacramenta celebranda tradidit* | *sui* is wanting on *carnis sanguinisque* (it is the Lord's **own** flesh and blood everywhere else in the chapter, e.g. @0338B *suae carnis et sanguinis sacramentum*), while *suis* attaches to *discipulis*. Possibly nothing; possibly a *sui* absorbed into *suis*. | apparent omission |

**Twenty-four oddities logged, zero marked.** Six are punctuation, eight are a real word that
reads wrong, three are case or number, two are an apparent omission, one is a fused word
division, one is a correlative, one is a capitalisation, one is a dittography (marked `[d:]`
only), one is a one-word Vulgate variant. **I make no claim about any of them.**

Three shapes are worth a reader's attention before the rest: **#20 (*intimis*)** because it is
the fused-word class the brief says no test can find; **#3, #21 and #22** because three lost
full stops inside eight columns is a pattern, not three accidents, and 9001 @0202D showed that
class going the digitization's way; and **#13 (*utile*)** because it is the one site where the
English says something a reader may want to correct, and the crux is where the alternative lives.

# STINT 0036 — chunks 0036–0040, cols 0340C–0356A

*Preserved verbatim from `cruces-0036.md`. Where it states a `[var:]` threshold of its own, §0.1 governs.*

# Cruces — 9000, *Glossa ordinaria*, **Evangelium secundum Lucam**, chunks 0036–0040

Stint 8 of eight: cols **0340C–0356A**, the passion and resurrection (capita XXII–XXIV) and the
end of the work. 5,866 Latin words, five chunks, 159 italic spans.

---

## (a) THE POLICY I APPLIED — stated before the findings, so it can be argued with

1. **No `[sic:]`, anywhere, for any reason.** This is the launch brief's §2 finding from John,
   and I treated it as absolute. Every suspect word in this range — a real word reading oddly,
   a non-word, a case or number that will not agree — is **rendered as printed, logged in §(e),
   and left unmarked**. I do not know whether any of them is Migne's type or Corpus Corporum's
   transcription of it, and I say so site by site rather than once in a preamble. My attribution
   guess carries no evidential weight and I have not offered one.
2. **No `[cj:]` either.** Pattern 18's bar is that the printed word makes the sentence assert
   something the author is arguing *against*. Four sites in this range are strong conjecture
   candidates (§e.2 *Galliaeus*, §e.10 *ibi* for *ubi*, §e.13 *cum parte potestatis*, §e.16
   *teruisse*) and **none** of them inverts a negation, a correlative or a claim: each leaves the
   sentence obscure, not false. All four are declined, with the conjecture named in §(e), which
   is where 18a says an uncertain reading belongs.
3. **`[var:]` is the one marker I fire, twice, and only where different MATTER stands in a quoted
   clause and a reader would take away something the Vulgate does not say.** Both rest on
   `sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt`, cited book chapter:verse, and both sit on a **clause**,
   not a word — the class standing 88 of 88 across three Glossa gospels.
   **Declined, explicitly and as a class:** every **one-word** substitution (*novit*/*cognovit*,
   *ad dexteram*/*a dextris*, *super*/*supra* class, *enim* dropped, *ergo* dropped); every
   **word-order-only** divergence (Rom 10:4, 2 Cor 5:16, Lk 24:24); every **truncation or
   abridgment cut** (1 Pet 2:21 loses *vobis relinquens exemplum*; the many lemmata cut mid-verse);
   every **orthographic** difference (*Hierusalem*/*Jerusalem*, *Syndone*/*sindone*,
   *coel-*/*cael-*); and every **grammatical adaptation of a quotation into the gloss's own
   syntax** (Jn 5:46 recast into the third person). A one-word `[var:]` on an unread column is
   the trap 9003's collation exposed, where the digitization *manufactured* the divergence a
   collation then dutifully found.
4. **Migne's wrong citation ADDRESSES take no marker and no correction.** Five notes in this range
   point at the wrong book, chapter or verse — `(Luc. XX)` on John 14:30, `(Joan. VIII)` on John
   5:46, `(Matth. XXVIII)` on Matt 18:20, `(Rom. XVIII)` on Rom 4:25, `(I Tim. III)` on 2 Tim
   3:12. `[n:]` contents are reproduced verbatim and untranslated; they are logged in §(e) as
   addresses, not as text defects.
5. **The inherited axes (brief §1) were applied from the first line, not reconciled afterwards**,
   and are censused in §(d).1. One prose exception to *usque ad* → "as far as" is declared there,
   with the Latin that forced it.
6. **The end-of-work anchor sequence is NOT reported as a defect.** Chunk 0040 runs `0354C`,
   `0354D`, `0355A`, `0356A`. Per the brief's §6.2, read at the plate this session: p. 183 divides
   **full-width and horizontally** — Luke's ending fills the top band of both columns (355A and
   356A), then a rule and the `EVANGELIUM SECUNDUM JOANNEM` title span the page, then John fills
   both columns below, so **355B sits below 356A on the paper**. Nothing is missing between 0355A
   and 0356A; no anchor was touched.

## (b) SPAN COUNT — 159 counted against 159 in the brief

I counted the `*…*` spans in my own Latin with the inventory script's own rule (pairs of
asterisks, per line, frontmatter stripped):

| chunk | spans |
|---|---|
| 0036 | 42 |
| 0037 | 39 |
| 0038 | 26 |
| 0039 | 43 |
| 0040 | 9 |
| **total** | **159** |

`data/briefs/9000-lemmata-0036.txt` states **159**. **They agree exactly**, and no line of the
Latin carries an odd number of asterisks (unclosed-span check: 0).

⚑ **But re-running the pre-scan per verse rather than against the whole file changed two marks,
and the mechanism is worth recording.** `lemma-inventory.mjs` normalises the entire Clementine
into **one string** and searches that, so a span can score ✓ by matching **across a verse
boundary**. Two spans in my range are ✓ in the brief and match no single verse:

- **[0340D] *Orabat dicens.*** — Lc 22:41 ends *positis genibus orabat,* and 22:42 opens
  *dicens : Pater, si vis*. The ✓ is the join of the two lines.
- **[0353A] *Spiritum videre. Et dixit eis.*** — Lc 24:37 ends *existimabant se spiritum videre*
  and 24:38 opens *Et dixit eis*. Same join.

**Both are innocent**: they are the recension's own splice of two consecutive verses it expounds
together, which is exactly what it does elsewhere with `etc.` So this is not a divergence — but
it is a **third way a ✓ can mean nothing**, alongside the two the brief already names (matching
some other verse, and the prefix bug fixed on 2026-09-03). Worth a line in the script's comments.

---

## (c) THE LEMMA DIVERGENCE LIST — all 159 spans, in order, with the reason for every decline

Verdicts: **AGREES** = the exact word sequence stands in the verse being glossed (per-verse
anchored search, not the whole-file test). **DIVERGES** = it does not, with the reason it took no
marker. Verse addresses are mine, from `clementine-flat.txt`; Migne's own `[n:]` addresses are
reproduced in the text verbatim and are not corrected.

### Chunk 0036 — cols 0340C–0343D (Luke 22:40–71) — 42 spans

1. [0340C] *Orate.* — AGREES Lc 22:40.
2. [0340C] *ne intretis in tentationem. Orate,* — **DIVERGES, splice — DECLINED.** Lc 22:40 reads
   *Orate ne intretis in tentationem*; the recension has just quoted *Orate* as the lemma and
   re-quotes the rest with *Orate,* trailing, to run into *id est*. Its own typography, not a
   variant. All the words are the verse's.
3. [0340C] *Et ipse,* — AGREES Lc 22:41.
4. [0340C] *Jactus est lapidis,* — AGREES Lc 22:41 (*quantum jactus est lapidis*, cut at the head).
5. [0340D] *finis legis est Christus omni credenti ad justitiam [n: (Rom. X)]* — **DIVERGES, word
   order and copula — DECLINED.** Vulg Rom 10:4 *Finis enim legis, Christus, ad justitiam omni
   credenti*: Migne drops *enim*, adds *est*, and reverses *ad justitiam omni credenti*. Nothing a
   reader takes away changes, and the gloss turns on *finis legis*, which stands. The English
   keeps Migne's order ("to everyone that believeth unto justice") so the divergence is visible.
6. [0340D] *Orabat dicens.* — **DIVERGES, splice across Lc 22:41/42 — DECLINED.** See §(b): the
   words are the two verses' own, joined at the address. ⚑ Marked ✓ in the brief.

8. [0340D] *Pater, si vis,* — AGREES Lc 22:42.
